But then I have to explain the grant money for the archives, and that reminds me of the shipping routes they used to bring the photographs over, and then I'm telling her about my grandmother's altar because she had this beautiful old map on it. \n\nAltars are so fascinating, aren't they? The way people arrange things... candles and little statues. My grandmother had these tall, white taper candles that she'd light every Sunday. They smelled like beeswax. I love the smell of beeswax, it's so much nicer than those artificial vanilla candles you buy at the mall. The mall is always so crowded now, teenagers just loitering around the food court. I remember when the food court first opened, it was such a big deal. We went and got these huge slices of pizza on paper plates. Paper plates are so convenient, but they're terrible for the environment, all that trash piling up in landfills. Landfills just stretch on for miles, don't they? \n\nAnyway, by the time I stop talking to my cousin, she just looks at me and nobody seems to have any idea what I meant to say in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special or unique status that makes you more important or powerful than others in a way that's not based on your accomplishments or talents?\n\nPatient: Important? Oh, no, not at all. I don't have any special power or anything like that. I'm just a regular woman trying to do my archiving work. The archives are important, of course, because history is so fragile, but I'm just the one organizing it. It's just moments captured on these little pieces of paper. \n\nMy father used to take photographs with this heavy silver camera he bought after the war. It had those big flashbulbs that would pop and leave a purple spot in your vision for ten minutes afterwards. We used to go to the beach in New Jersey every summer, and he'd take pictures of us building sandcastles. The sand there was always so hot on your bare feet, you had to run all the way down to the water just to keep your toes from burning. The water was freezing, though, just shockingly cold. I used to find these tiny, perfect little white seashells in the tide pools and keep them in an old jelly jar on my bedroom windowsill. Jelly jars used to be so thick, the glass was practically unbreakable. You could drop them on the linoleum and they'd just bounce. Now everything comes in plastic. Plastic just feels so cheap, doesn't it? And it's everywhere now, just floating out there in the ocean...\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your thoughts are connected in a logical way when you're talking, or do they seem disjointed to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\nmanifestation: \"When my cousin asks a simple question about a bill or an appointment, I start explaining the grant, then the shipping routes, then my grandmother’s altar, and by the time I stop talking nobody seems to know what I meant.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... didn't I just answer this? I feel like we just went over this a moment ago. 

Like I was saying, to me, everything connects perfectly fine. But I know it doesn't always sound that way to other people. I was just telling you about my cousin—if she asks me a simple question about paying a bill or an upcoming appointment, I'll start explaining the archive grant to her, and then that brings up the old shipping routes, and then I'm talking about my grandmother's altar. And by the time I stop talking, she just looks at me and nobody seems to know what I meant in the first place. 

But those shipping routes really were quite dangerous back then, you know. The storms out on the Atlantic could just swallow a boat whole. I went on a boat once, a ferry across to Staten Island. The wind was so fierce it blew my scarf right off my neck and into the water. It was a beautiful silk scarf, hand-painted with little blue flowers. Silk is so delicate, you really have to hand-wash it in cold water in the sink. If you put it in the washing machine, it just ruins the fibers completely. Washing machines are so loud anyway, mine has this terrible spin cycle that shakes the whole kitchen floor. It makes the floorboards rattle so much I think the tea cups are going to fall right out of the cupboards. Cupboards in those old apartments were built so solid, though, real wood, not like the particle board they use today...

He always wore these heavy tweed jackets that smelled just like pipe tobacco and... maybe peppermint? I actually tried to find a tobacco exactly like that for my ex-husband years ago. We had to drive all the way out to this tiny little shop in Connecticut to look for it. It rained the entire trip, just poured. Have you ever been to Connecticut in the fall? The leaves are stunning, but the roads get so incredibly slick, and the windshield wipers on that old Volvo we had were just completely shot...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having trouble staying on topic when you talk, or is there something that connects your thoughts about the voices, the archive, and your past experiences?\n\nPatient: I suppose to me, it all connects perfectly. But other people do seem to get confused when I talk. Like, my cousin will come over and ask me a very simple question, just about a water bill or what time an appointment is. And to give her the full answer, I naturally start explaining the grant, which leads into the maritime shipping routes. The shipping routes are actually fascinating, they used to bring in all this heavy, gorgeous mahogany wood from overseas. \n\nMy grandmother had this stunning mahogany altar in her front room. She kept these little brass bells on it, and I used to polish them for her with this lemon oil. It smelled exactly like those hard lemon candies you can buy in the glass jars at the movie theater. I haven't been to a picture show in a decade, the floors are always so terribly sticky and the previews are too loud. \n\nBut... anyway, I'll be explaining all of this to my cousin, and by the time I finally stop talking, she just stares at me. Nobody ever seems to know what I meant or how I got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these voices or your way of thinking are causing you any problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose they are causing quite a bit of trouble. I've completely lost the trust of my colleagues, and I've missed so many board meetings lately. And the bills... I just can't seem to keep track of them. My cousin has had to step in, and even one of the museum administrators, just to help me manage my appointments and money because I can't do it safely on my own anymore. \n\nMoney is such a strange concept, isn't it? I remember when I was a little girl, my father used to give me a shiny silver half-dollar every Sunday if I helped him wash the car. He had this beautiful, cherry-red convertible. The leather seats would get so hot in the summer sun, you could practically fry an egg on them. I always loved eggs, especially poached, but you have to get the water temperature just right or the whites get all wispy and ruin the whole thing. I tried to teach my ex-husband how to poach an egg once, but he was terribly impatient. He could never just stand and wait for things to happen naturally...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been experiencing extreme highs or lows in your mood lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. There are these days where I just feel absolutely radiant, like I'm glowing from the inside out and I've been chosen for something incredibly important. But then... if anyone questions my plan, or tells me I need to slow down, I just become furious. It's like they're trying to completely erase me. \n\nErasing things... you know, I used to have this wonderful pink pearl eraser when I was in grade school. I loved the way it smelled, sort of dusty and sweet. I used to draw these elaborate floor plans for mansions on my math homework, and I'd have to erase them before the teacher saw. Mr. Henderson was my math teacher, and he always wore these terrible plaid ties. They clashed horribly with his shirts. I don't understand how people can just ignore color theory like that. Color is so fundamental to how we experience the world, don't you think? Like the blue of the ocean—it's never just one blue, it's a thousand different shades of cerulean and navy and teal, all shifting with the tide...\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your periods of feeling radiant and furious come and go, or are they more constant?\n\nPatient: Well, since I retired, I suppose they've been mostly constant. These past months have been completely dominated by these long stretches where I feel so incredibly driven, like I've been chosen for this grand purpose, and I just can't slow down. I get so irritable when I have to stop. Even on the days where I feel quieter and more withdrawn, that driven feeling is still humming underneath it all. \n\nHumming... you know, my refrigerator has been making this awful humming noise lately. It's an older model, one of those big white ones with the freezer on the bottom. I've always preferred the freezer on the bottom, it makes so much more sense for your back. Why should you have to bend over to get a head of lettuce? Lettuce is so delicate, anyway. If you leave it in the crisper drawer too long, it gets all slimy and sad. I used to grow my own butter lettuce in a little window box when I lived in my first apartment. It was a charming little place, right above a bakery. Every morning at 4 AM, the smell of fresh sourdough would wake me up. Sourdough is fascinating, isn't it? The way the wild yeast just lives in the air, waiting to be captured...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices you hear are actually trying to communicate something real to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I absolutely do believe they're real. It's the whole reason I'm here, really. I keep hearing these very clear voices, mostly when I'm alone in my apartment, or sometimes when I'm walking through the empty rooms at the museum. It sounds like women, whispering right from behind the walls, and they're telling me exactly which photographs belong in the archive. \n\nArchives are such dusty places, aren't they? I remember when I was doing the research for my senior thesis in college, I spent hours down in the basement of the university library. It always smelled like old paper and binding glue. They used to make glue out of horse hooves, you know. It seems so barbaric now, but I suppose people had to use whatever they had. Horses are such magnificent creatures. My grandfather had a dapple gray mare named Clementine. She loved peppermints. I used to keep those little red and white striped candies in my pockets just for her. The wrappers would always get stuck in the lint at the bottom of my coat pocket. Lint is so strange, isn't it? Just all the little bits of our lives rubbing off and collecting in the dark...\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do. They give me very specific instructions. Just the other day... well, it was at the postponed museum program. I felt completely compelled to arrange all the chairs into this sort of island map. I just started moving them around, and then I had to stand right at the front with my arms lifted up. Leaving or sitting down just felt like I would be disobeying the message I had been given, so I had to stay like that. \n\nIslands are so fascinating, aren't they? Completely surrounded by water, cut off from everything else. I read once that there are islands in the Pacific where the animals evolved completely differently because they were so isolated. Like those giant tortoises in the Galapagos. Imagine living for over a hundred years, just slowly walking around with your house on your back. My niece has a turtle, a little red-eared slider named Sheldon. She keeps him in this glass tank with a little plastic palm tree. Plastic plants always look so depressing to me. They gather dust so quickly, and you can never really get them clean. I tried washing a plastic fern once in the kitchen sink, but the leaves just kept popping off. It's much better to have real plants, even if you have to remember to water them. I had a beautiful spider plant for years, but it died when the heating in my building broke one winter...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special role or mission that the voices are guiding you towards?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. It's not just the voices guiding me, it's... I'm convinced the museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone. They know my Caribbean archive will completely change how the galleries tell the truth. No one has shown me a single piece of evidence that proves otherwise, so I know they're listening. \n\nListening... you know, I used to love listening to those old radio shows when I was a little girl. We had this big wooden Zenith radio in the living room. My father would tune it to these mystery programs on Sunday nights. We'd all sit around the fireplace, just staring at the glowing dial. Fireplaces are so wonderful, but the ash gets everywhere. I remember sweeping up soot for hours. My mother had this beautiful brass ash bucket, but it was so heavy to carry out to the garden. She used to dump the ashes around the rose bushes. She said it made the blooms brighter. She had this one bush, a Peace rose, that produced the most enormous yellow and pink flowers. I tried growing roses once, but the aphids got to them. Little green bugs, just sucking the life out of the stems... I bought ladybugs to eat them, you can order them right through the mail in a little mesh bag. It's so strange that you can just put living creatures in the post...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special or unique status that sets you apart from other people?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I have these days where I just feel so... radiant, you know? Like I've been chosen for this incredible purpose. But then, if anyone tries to question my plan or tells me I need to slow down, I just become absolutely furious. It's like they're trying to erase me, to make me disappear entirely. \n\nDisappearing... it reminds me of this magic trick my brother used to do when we were kids. He had this little wooden box with a false bottom, painted bright red. He'd put a silk handkerchief inside, tap it with his plastic wand, and poof! The handkerchief was gone. He got that magic kit for his tenth birthday. We had a party in the backyard with paper streamers strung up between the oak trees. Oak trees drop so many acorns in the fall, don't they? It's like walking on marbles if you're not careful. I slipped on an acorn once and twisted my ankle right before my high school graduation. I had to walk across the stage with a terrible limp. My mother bought me these beautiful white patent leather shoes for the ceremony, but I couldn't even wear the right one because my ankle was so swollen. Swelling is just your body's way of protecting itself, I suppose. Like a cushion... I always liked those velvet cushions you see in old theaters, the really deep red ones that feel so soft when you run your hand over them...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special or unique status that sets you apart from other people in a way that's more than just being talented or accomplished?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not a prophet or a queen or anything like that. I don't have some magical title. Prophets always had such a terrible time of it anyway, wandering out in the desert for years. The desert gets so incredibly cold at night, you know. People always think it's just hot sand and sunshine, but the temperature just plummets as soon as the sun goes down. \n\nMy husband and I drove through Arizona years ago, and I actually had to buy this heavy wool sweater at a little roadside trading post just to keep warm. It was hand-knit with these beautiful geometric patterns, deep reds and a bright turquoise color. Turquoise is such a fascinating stone, really. I used to have a heavy silver ring with a big oval piece of turquoise in the center, but it slipped right off my finger while I was washing dishes and went straight down the drain. The plumber charged an absolute fortune to come out and look for it. Two hundred dollars just to unscrew a rusty pipe under the sink! You'd think they were surgeons with the rates they charge, carrying those heavy metal toolboxes around. My father had a toolbox just like that, painted dark green, and it always smelled like machine oil and sawdust...\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the voices you hear or the messages you receive might not be real?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think that's possible. They're as real as you sitting right there. I hear them clearly, just like... like the sound of the ocean when you hold a seashell up to your ear. You know, we used to go to Cape Cod every summer when the children were little, and we'd walk along the beach looking for shells. My daughter found this perfectly intact conch shell once, beautiful pink on the inside. She kept it on her nightstand for years. Nightstands are so useful, aren't they? I have this lovely antique cherry wood one with a little drawer where I keep my reading glasses. I can never seem to find my reading glasses when I need them, though. I must have three pairs scattered around the house, but the moment I sit down with a magazine, poof, they're gone. I was reading this fascinating article the other day about how they make glass, melting sand down at those incredibly high temperatures. It's amazing what they can do with heat. My oven has been acting up lately, actually, burning the bottoms of all my cookies...\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused on a single task or idea when you're talking or working on something?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's just impossible lately. If I try to read a letter, or, you know, just sit down and talk with my cousin... she comes over on Tuesdays, usually... but the moment we start talking, a siren will go off outside, or I'll hear the refrigerator hum, or I'll see a bright color in a brochure on the table, and I just... I lose the thread almost immediately. It's like my mind just gets pulled right out the window. \n\nWindows are so drafty in my house. It's an older house, built in the twenties, I think. They used that beautiful wavy glass back then, the kind that distorts everything just a little bit when you look through it. I always loved looking out the window when it rained, watching the drops race each other down the glass. We had this terrible storm last spring that knocked a branch right through the living room window. Shattered everywhere. It took the repairman three weeks to come out and fix it, and he left muddy boot prints all over my good rug. It's a Persian rug, hand-woven. The colors are mostly deep blues and golds... gold is such a soft metal, isn't it? You can practically bend it with your bare hands if it's pure enough. My grandmother had a pure gold locket she wore every day of her life...\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a special or unique status that sets you apart from other people in a way that makes you more important or powerful than others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't feel like I'm more important or powerful than anyone else. I'm just me, you know? I don't have any grand powers. I can't even get the dandelions out of my front lawn. They just keep coming back, no matter how much I pull them. Dandelions are actually edible, you know. I read a book once about foraging, and they said you can make wine out of the flowers. Wine making seems like such an exact science, all those barrels and the fermenting process. We took a tour of a vineyard in Napa Valley for our anniversary one year. The smell of the grapes was so sweet, almost overpowering. They had these massive oak barrels stacked to the ceiling. Oak is such a sturdy wood, perfect for furniture. My dining room table is solid oak, weighs an absolute ton. We had to hire three men just to get it through the front door when we moved in. Moving is always such a nightmare, packing up your whole life into little cardboard boxes...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question whether the voices or instructions you receive might be a product of your own mind rather than an external reality?\n\nPatient: Well, no, how could they be my own mind? They're real women. I hear them so clearly in my apartment, and... and sometimes when I'm walking through empty museum rooms. They whisper from right behind the walls, telling me exactly which photographs belong in the archive. Archiving is such delicate work, you know. You have to wear those little white cotton gloves so the oils from your fingers don't ruin the paper. I had a pair of white gloves just like that for my first communion. My mother bought them at a department store downtown... Gimbel's, I think it was. Gimbel's had the most wonderful escalator, all shiny brass and wood. I used to ride it up and down while my mother looked at the perfume counters. Perfume gives me such a terrible headache now, especially those heavy floral ones. Roses are lovely in a garden, but bottled up, it's just too much. I tried planting a rose bush last spring, but the aphids got to it almost immediately. Little green bugs just covering the stems...\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you hear and the way your thoughts are connecting are related to your feelings of being radiant and furious, or are they separate things?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't think they're exactly the same thing. I mean, yes, lately I've felt so... so bright and electric, and the furious energy is there, but the voices don't just happen when I feel like that. Actually, there was a period just a little while ago, maybe for a few weeks, where I didn't feel energized or radiant at all. I was just perfectly normal, just quiet. But I still heard the women whispering in my apartment. And I knew, I absolutely knew, that the arts people were still monitoring my phone calls. \n\nPhones used to be so heavy, remember? The old rotary ones, the black ones. My parents had one in the hallway, and the cord was so long you could stretch it all the way into the kitchen if you wanted privacy. But it would get all tangled up like a corkscrew. Corkscrews are so tricky to use sometimes. I always break the cork when I try to open a bottle. My husband used to do it so easily, just a quick twist and pop. He had these wonderful, strong hands. He built a birdhouse once for the backyard, painted it bright red. The cardinals just loved it. Cardinals are such beautiful birds, the way that red pops against the snow in the winter. Snow is so peaceful when it first falls, before the plows come and turn it all gray and slushy...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected in a logical way when you're talking, or do they seem disjointed to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I think they make perfect sense to me, but... but I suppose other people don't always follow along. Like, my cousin will ask me a very simple question, just about a doctor's appointment or paying a utility bill, and I'll start trying to explain it. But then I have to explain the grant money for the archives, and that reminds me of the shipping routes they used to bring the photographs over, and then I'm telling her about my grandmother's altar because she had this beautiful old map on it. \n\nAltars are so fascinating, aren't they? The way people arrange things... candles and little statues. My grandmother had these tall, white taper candles that she'd light every Sunday. They smelled like beeswax. I love the smell of beeswax, it's so much nicer than those artificial vanilla candles you buy at the mall. The mall is always so crowded now, teenagers just loitering around the food court. I remember when the food court first opened, it was such a big deal. We went and got these huge slices of pizza on paper plates. Paper plates are so convenient, but they're terrible for the environment, all that trash piling up in landfills. Landfills just stretch on for miles, don't they? \n\nAnyway, by the time I stop talking to my cousin, she just looks at me and nobody seems to have any idea what I meant to say in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special or unique status that makes you more important or powerful than others in a way that's not based on your accomplishments or talents?\n\nPatient: Important? Oh, no, not at all. I don't have any special power or anything like that. I'm just a regular woman trying to do my archiving work. The archives are important, of course, because history is so fragile, but I'm just the one organizing it. It's just moments captured on these little pieces of paper. \n\nMy father used to take photographs with this heavy silver camera he bought after the war. It had those big flashbulbs that would pop and leave a purple spot in your vision for ten minutes afterwards. We used to go to the beach in New Jersey every summer, and he'd take pictures of us building sandcastles. The sand there was always so hot on your bare feet, you had to run all the way down to the water just to keep your toes from burning. The water was freezing, though, just shockingly cold. I used to find these tiny, perfect little white seashells in the tide pools and keep them in an old jelly jar on my bedroom windowsill. Jelly jars used to be so thick, the glass was practically unbreakable. You could drop them on the linoleum and they'd just bounce. Now everything comes in plastic. Plastic just feels so cheap, doesn't it? And it's everywhere now, just floating out there in the ocean...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected in a logical way when you're talking, or do they seem disjointed to you?\n\nPatient: I... didn't I just answer this? I feel like we just went over this a moment ago. \n\nLike I was saying, to me, everything connects perfectly fine. But I know it doesn't always sound that way to other people. I was just telling you about my cousin—if she asks me a simple question about paying a bill or an upcoming appointment, I'll start explaining the archive grant to her, and then that brings up the old shipping routes, and then I'm talking about my grandmother's altar. And by the time I stop talking, she just looks at me and nobody seems to know what I meant in the first place. \n\nBut those shipping routes really were quite dangerous back then, you know. The storms out on the Atlantic could just swallow a boat whole. I went on a boat once, a ferry across to Staten Island. The wind was so fierce it blew my scarf right off my neck and into the water. It was a beautiful silk scarf, hand-painted with little blue flowers. Silk is so delicate, you really have to hand-wash it in cold water in the sink. If you put it in the washing machine, it just ruins the fibers completely. Washing machines are so loud anyway, mine has this terrible spin cycle that shakes the whole kitchen floor. It makes the floorboards rattle so much I think the tea cups are going to fall right out of the cupboards. Cupboards in those old apartments were built so solid, though, real wood, not like the particle board they use today...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, energetic, yes. I get these days... sometimes it stretches on, and I just feel radiant. Like I'm chosen, you know? Like I'm glowing from the inside out and I have this incredible plan for the archives. I can work for hours and hours. But then, if someone questions my plan, or if my cousin tells me I need to slow down, I become absolutely furious. It's like they're trying to erase me. Erasing things is so dangerous, especially with historical documents. You use those little pink erasers, the ones that come on the ends of pencils, and they leave those terrible pink smudges all over the paper. Pencils are better than pens for archiving, though, because ink can bleed right through the paper. I bought this beautiful fountain pen once, it had a gold nib and real blue-black ink. The ink came in this little glass bottle with a cork stopper. Cork is fascinating, isn't it? It comes from the bark of a tree, they just peel it right off. I saw a documentary about it on PBS once. They have such good shows on PBS, the nature ones with the British narrators. Their voices are just so soothing, like a warm cup of tea on a rainy afternoon. Rain always makes my joints ache a little, though, right in the knees. It's the barometric pressure dropping...

Leaving or sitting down just felt like I would be disobeying the message I had been given, so I had to stay like that. \n\nIslands are so fascinating, aren't they? Completely surrounded by water, cut off from everything else. I read once that there are islands in the Pacific where the animals evolved completely differently because they were so isolated. Like those giant tortoises in the Galapagos. Imagine living for over a hundred years, just slowly walking around with your house on your back. My niece has a turtle, a little red-eared slider named Sheldon. She keeps him in this glass tank with a little plastic palm tree. Plastic plants always look so depressing to me. They gather dust so quickly, and you can never really get them clean. I tried washing a plastic fern once in the kitchen sink, but the leaves just kept popping off. It's much better to have real plants, even if you have to remember to water them. I had a beautiful spider plant for years, but it died when the heating in my building broke one winter...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special role or mission that the voices are guiding you towards?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. They know my Caribbean archive will completely change how the galleries tell the truth. Fireplaces are so wonderful, but the ash gets everywhere. I remember sweeping up soot for hours. My mother had this beautiful brass ash bucket, but it was so heavy to carry out to the garden. She used to dump the ashes around the rose bushes. She said it made the blooms brighter. She had this one bush, a Peace rose, that produced the most enormous yellow and pink flowers. He got that magic kit for his tenth birthday. We had a party in the backyard with paper streamers strung up between the oak trees. Oak trees drop so many acorns in the fall, don't they? But it would get all tangled up like a corkscrew. Corkscrews are so tricky to use sometimes. I always break the cork when I try to open a bottle. My husband used to do it so easily, just a quick twist and pop. He had these wonderful, strong hands. He built a birdhouse once for the backyard, painted it bright red. The cardinals just loved it. Snow is so peaceful when it first falls, before the plows come and turn it all gray and slushy...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected in a logical way when you're talking, or do they seem disjointed to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I think they make perfect sense to me, but... but I suppose other people don't always follow along. The way people arrange things... candles and little statues. My grandmother had these tall, white taper candles that she'd light every Sunday. They smelled like beeswax. I love the smell of beeswax, it's so much nicer than those artificial vanilla candles you buy at the mall. The mall is always so crowded now, teenagers just loitering around the food court. I remember when the food court first opened, it was such a big deal. We went and got these huge slices of pizza on paper plates. Paper plates are so convenient, but they're terrible for the environment, all that trash piling up in landfills. Landfills just stretch on for miles, don't they? \n\nAnyway, by the time I stop talking to my cousin, she just looks at me and nobody seems to have any idea what I meant to say in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special or unique status that makes you more important or powerful than others in a way that's not based on your accomplishments or talents?\n\nPatient: Important? Oh, no, not at all. I don't have any special power or anything like that. I'm just a regular woman trying to do my archiving work. The archives are important, of course, because history is so fragile, but I'm just the one organizing it. It's just moments captured on these little pieces of paper. \n\nMy father used to take photographs with this heavy silver camera he bought after the war. It had those big flashbulbs that would pop and leave a purple spot in your vision for ten minutes afterwards. We used to go to the beach in New Jersey every summer, and he'd take pictures of us building sandcastles. The sand there was always so hot on your bare feet, you had to run all the way down to the water just to keep your toes from burning. The water was freezing, though, just shockingly cold. I used to find these tiny, perfect little white seashells in the tide pools and keep them in an old jelly jar on my bedroom windowsill. And by the time I stop talking, she just looks at me and nobody seems to know what I meant in the first place. \n\nBut those shipping routes really were quite dangerous back then, you know. It was a beautiful silk scarf, hand-painted with little blue flowers. Silk is so delicate, you really have to hand-wash it in cold water in the sink. If you put it in the washing machine, it just ruins the fibers completely. Washing machines are so loud anyway, mine has this terrible spin cycle that shakes the whole kitchen floor. It makes the floorboards rattle so much I think the tea cups are going to fall right out of the cupboards. Cupboards in those old apartments were built so solid, though, real wood, not like the particle board they use today...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt extremely energetic or irritable for an extended period, like days or weeks, without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Well, energetic, yes. You use those little pink erasers, the ones that come on the ends of pencils, and they leave those terrible pink smudges all over the paper. Pencils are better than pens for archiving, though, because ink can bleed right through the paper. I bought this beautiful fountain pen once, it had a gold nib and real blue-black ink. Rain always makes my joints ache a little, though, right in the knees. It's the barometric pressure dropping...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it really depends. Sometimes I can sit down and just focus, but other times... it's like everything is pulling at me at once. If I try to read a letter for the archives, or even just sit and talk with my cousin, the slightest thing will pull me away. A siren going by outside, or the hum of the refrigerator kicking on, or even just a bright color on a brochure sitting on the table... I just lose the thread almost immediately. 

Brochures are always so glossy, aren't they? They use that heavy coated paper so the colors pop. I remember picking up a brochure for a botanical garden once, it had the most vibrant picture of a blue hydrangea on the front. Hydrangeas are fascinating because the color of the flowers changes depending on the acidity of the soil. If you put coffee grounds in the dirt, they turn blue, but if the soil is alkaline, they turn pink. My mother grew the most beautiful pink roses along the side of our house. They had thorns like little daggers, though. You had to wear these thick leather gloves just to prune them. Leather always smells so distinct, like an old library or a saddle shop. I bought a leather satchel at a flea market years ago to carry my notebooks, but the strap broke right in the middle of a crosswalk. The light changed and all the cars started honking, and I was just standing there trying to gather up all my loose papers before they blew away in the wind...

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is... I keep hearing these very clear voices. It's mostly in my apartment, but sometimes when I'm out, too, like in empty museum rooms. It sounds like women whispering from right behind the walls, telling me exactly which photographs belong in the archive. \n\nThe archive... you know, I actually got my bachelor's degree in art history. I've always had an eye for composition. My favorite professor, Dr. Aris, he had this massive collection of original silver gelatin prints. He always wore these heavy tweed jackets that smelled just like pipe tobacco and... maybe peppermint? I actually tried to find a tobacco exactly like that for my ex-husband years ago. We had to drive all the way out to this tiny little shop in Connecticut to look for it. It rained the entire trip, just poured. Have you ever been to Connecticut in the fall? The leaves are stunning, but the roads get so incredibly slick, and the windshield wipers on that old Volvo we had were just completely shot...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having trouble staying on topic when you talk, or is there something that connects your thoughts about the voices, the archive, and your past experiences?\n\nPatient: I suppose to me, it all connects perfectly. But other people do seem to get confused when I talk. Like, my cousin will come over and ask me a very simple question, just about a water bill or what time an appointment is. And to give her the full answer, I naturally start explaining the grant, which leads into the maritime shipping routes. The shipping routes are actually fascinating, they used to bring in all this heavy, gorgeous mahogany wood from overseas. \n\nMy grandmother had this stunning mahogany altar in her front room. She kept these little brass bells on it, and I used to polish them for her with this lemon oil. It smelled exactly like those hard lemon candies you can buy in the glass jars at the movie theater. I haven't been to a picture show in a decade, the floors are always so terribly sticky and the previews are too loud. \n\nBut... anyway, I'll be explaining all of this to my cousin, and by the time I finally stop talking, she just stares at me. Nobody ever seems to know what I meant or how I got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these voices or your way of thinking are causing you any problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose they are causing quite a bit of trouble. I've completely lost the trust of my colleagues, and I've missed so many board meetings lately. And the bills... I just can't seem to keep track of them. My cousin has had to step in, and even one of the museum administrators, just to help me manage my appointments and money because I can't do it safely on my own anymore. \n\nMoney is such a strange concept, isn't it? I remember when I was a little girl, my father used to give me a shiny silver half-dollar every Sunday if I helped him wash the car. He had this beautiful, cherry-red convertible. The leather seats would get so hot in the summer sun, you could practically fry an egg on them. I always loved eggs, especially poached, but you have to get the water temperature just right or the whites get all wispy and ruin the whole thing. I tried to teach my ex-husband how to poach an egg once, but he was terribly impatient. He could never just stand and wait for things to happen naturally...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been experiencing extreme highs or lows in your mood lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. There are these days where I just feel absolutely radiant, like I'm glowing from the inside out and I've been chosen for something incredibly important. But then... if anyone questions my plan, or tells me I need to slow down, I just become furious. It's like they're trying to completely erase me. \n\nErasing things... you know, I used to have this wonderful pink pearl eraser when I was in grade school. I loved the way it smelled, sort of dusty and sweet. I used to draw these elaborate floor plans for mansions on my math homework, and I'd have to erase them before the teacher saw. Mr. Henderson was my math teacher, and he always wore these terrible plaid ties. They clashed horribly with his shirts. I don't understand how people can just ignore color theory like that. Color is so fundamental to how we experience the world, don't you think? Like the blue of the ocean—it's never just one blue, it's a thousand different shades of cerulean and navy and teal, all shifting with the tide...\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your periods of feeling radiant and furious come and go, or are they more constant?\n\nPatient: Well, since I retired, I suppose they've been mostly constant. These past months have been completely dominated by these long stretches where I feel so incredibly driven, like I've been chosen for this grand purpose, and I just can't slow down. I get so irritable when I have to stop. Even on the days where I feel quieter and more withdrawn, that driven feeling is still humming underneath it all. \n\nHumming... you know, my refrigerator has been making this awful humming noise lately. It's an older model, one of those big white ones with the freezer on the bottom. I've always preferred the freezer on the bottom, it makes so much more sense for your back. Why should you have to bend over to get a head of lettuce? Lettuce is so delicate, anyway. If you leave it in the crisper drawer too long, it gets all slimy and sad. I used to grow my own butter lettuce in a little window box when I lived in my first apartment. It was a charming little place, right above a bakery. Every morning at 4 AM, the smell of fresh sourdough would wake me up. Sourdough is fascinating, isn't it? The way the wild yeast just lives in the air, waiting to be captured...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices you hear are actually trying to communicate something real to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I absolutely do believe they're real. It's the whole reason I'm here, really. I keep hearing these very clear voices, mostly when I'm alone in my apartment, or sometimes when I'm walking through the empty rooms at the museum. It sounds like women, whispering right from behind the walls, and they're telling me exactly which photographs belong in the archive. \n\nArchives are such dusty places, aren't they? I remember when I was doing the research for my senior thesis in college, I spent hours down in the basement of the university library. It always smelled like old paper and binding glue. They used to make glue out of horse hooves, you know. It seems so barbaric now, but I suppose people had to use whatever they had. Horses are such magnificent creatures. My grandfather had a dapple gray mare named Clementine. She loved peppermints. I used to keep those little red and white striped candies in my pockets just for her. The wrappers would always get stuck in the lint at the bottom of my coat pocket. Lint is so strange, isn't it? Just all the little bits of our lives rubbing off and collecting in the dark...\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do. They give me very specific instructions. Just the other day... well, it was at the postponed museum program. I felt completely compelled to arrange all the chairs into this sort of island map. I just started moving them around, and then I had to stand right at the front with my arms lifted up. Leaving or sitting down just felt like I would be disobeying the message I had been given, so I had to stay like that. \n\nIslands are so fascinating, aren't they? Completely surrounded by water, cut off from everything else. I read once that there are islands in the Pacific where the animals evolved completely differently because they were so isolated. Like those giant tortoises in the Galapagos. Imagine living for over a hundred years, just slowly walking around with your house on your back. My niece has a turtle, a little red-eared slider named Sheldon. She keeps him in this glass tank with a little plastic palm tree. Plastic plants always look so depressing to me. They gather dust so quickly, and you can never really get them clean. I tried washing a plastic fern once in the kitchen sink, but the leaves just kept popping off. It's much better to have real plants, even if you have to remember to water them. I had a beautiful spider plant for years, but it died when the heating in my building broke one winter...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special role or mission that the voices are guiding you towards?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. It's not just the voices guiding me, it's... I'm convinced the museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone. They know my Caribbean archive will completely change how the galleries tell the truth. No one has shown me a single piece of evidence that proves otherwise, so I know they're listening. \n\nListening... you know, I used to love listening to those old radio shows when I was a little girl. We had this big wooden Zenith radio in the living room. My father would tune it to these mystery programs on Sunday nights. We'd all sit around the fireplace, just staring at the glowing dial. Fireplaces are so wonderful, but the ash gets everywhere. I remember sweeping up soot for hours. My mother had this beautiful brass ash bucket, but it was so heavy to carry out to the garden. She used to dump the ashes around the rose bushes. She said it made the blooms brighter. She had this one bush, a Peace rose, that produced the most enormous yellow and pink flowers. I tried growing roses once, but the aphids got to them. Little green bugs, just sucking the life out of the stems... I bought ladybugs to eat them, you can order them right through the mail in a little mesh bag. It's so strange that you can just put living creatures in the post...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special or unique status that sets you apart from other people?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I have these days where I just feel so... radiant, you know? Like I've been chosen for this incredible purpose. But then, if anyone tries to question my plan or tells me I need to slow down, I just become absolutely furious. It's like they're trying to erase me, to make me disappear entirely. \n\nDisappearing... it reminds me of this magic trick my brother used to do when we were kids. He had this little wooden box with a false bottom, painted bright red. He'd put a silk handkerchief inside, tap it with his plastic wand, and poof! The handkerchief was gone. He got that magic kit for his tenth birthday. We had a party in the backyard with paper streamers strung up between the oak trees. Oak trees drop so many acorns in the fall, don't they? It's like walking on marbles if you're not careful. I slipped on an acorn once and twisted my ankle right before my high school graduation. I had to walk across the stage with a terrible limp. My mother bought me these beautiful white patent leather shoes for the ceremony, but I couldn't even wear the right one because my ankle was so swollen. Swelling is just your body's way of protecting itself, I suppose. Like a cushion... I always liked those velvet cushions you see in old theaters, the really deep red ones that feel so soft when you run your hand over them...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special or unique status that sets you apart from other people in a way that's more than just being talented or accomplished?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not a prophet or a queen or anything like that. I don't have some magical title. Prophets always had such a terrible time of it anyway, wandering out in the desert for years. The desert gets so incredibly cold at night, you know. People always think it's just hot sand and sunshine, but the temperature just plummets as soon as the sun goes down. \n\nMy husband and I drove through Arizona years ago, and I actually had to buy this heavy wool sweater at a little roadside trading post just to keep warm. It was hand-knit with these beautiful geometric patterns, deep reds and a bright turquoise color. Turquoise is such a fascinating stone, really. I used to have a heavy silver ring with a big oval piece of turquoise in the center, but it slipped right off my finger while I was washing dishes and went straight down the drain. The plumber charged an absolute fortune to come out and look for it. Two hundred dollars just to unscrew a rusty pipe under the sink! You'd think they were surgeons with the rates they charge, carrying those heavy metal toolboxes around. My father had a toolbox just like that, painted dark green, and it always smelled like machine oil and sawdust...\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the voices you hear or the messages you receive might not be real?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think that's possible. They're as real as you sitting right there. I hear them clearly, just like... like the sound of the ocean when you hold a seashell up to your ear. You know, we used to go to Cape Cod every summer when the children were little, and we'd walk along the beach looking for shells. My daughter found this perfectly intact conch shell once, beautiful pink on the inside. She kept it on her nightstand for years. Nightstands are so useful, aren't they? I have this lovely antique cherry wood one with a little drawer where I keep my reading glasses. I can never seem to find my reading glasses when I need them, though. I must have three pairs scattered around the house, but the moment I sit down with a magazine, poof, they're gone. I was reading this fascinating article the other day about how they make glass, melting sand down at those incredibly high temperatures. It's amazing what they can do with heat. My oven has been acting up lately, actually, burning the bottoms of all my cookies...\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused on a single task or idea when you're talking or working on something?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's just impossible lately. If I try to read a letter, or, you know, just sit down and talk with my cousin... she comes over on Tuesdays, usually... but the moment we start talking, a siren will go off outside, or I'll hear the refrigerator hum, or I'll see a bright color in a brochure on the table, and I just... I lose the thread almost immediately. It's like my mind just gets pulled right out the window. \n\nWindows are so drafty in my house. It's an older house, built in the twenties, I think. They used that beautiful wavy glass back then, the kind that distorts everything just a little bit when you look through it. I always loved looking out the window when it rained, watching the drops race each other down the glass. We had this terrible storm last spring that knocked a branch right through the living room window. Shattered everywhere. It took the repairman three weeks to come out and fix it, and he left muddy boot prints all over my good rug. It's a Persian rug, hand-woven. The colors are mostly deep blues and golds... gold is such a soft metal, isn't it? You can practically bend it with your bare hands if it's pure enough. My grandmother had a pure gold locket she wore every day of her life...\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a special or unique status that sets you apart from other people in a way that makes you more important or powerful than others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't feel like I'm more important or powerful than anyone else. I'm just me, you know? I don't have any grand powers. I can't even get the dandelions out of my front lawn. They just keep coming back, no matter how much I pull them. Dandelions are actually edible, you know. I read a book once about foraging, and they said you can make wine out of the flowers. Wine making seems like such an exact science, all those barrels and the fermenting process. We took a tour of a vineyard in Napa Valley for our anniversary one year. The smell of the grapes was so sweet, almost overpowering. They had these massive oak barrels stacked to the ceiling. Oak is such a sturdy wood, perfect for furniture. My dining room table is solid oak, weighs an absolute ton. We had to hire three men just to get it through the front door when we moved in. Moving is always such a nightmare, packing up your whole life into little cardboard boxes...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question whether the voices or instructions you receive might be a product of your own mind rather than an external reality?\n\nPatient: Well, no, how could they be my own mind? They're real women. I hear them so clearly in my apartment, and... and sometimes when I'm walking through empty museum rooms. They whisper from right behind the walls, telling me exactly which photographs belong in the archive. Archiving is such delicate work, you know. You have to wear those little white cotton gloves so the oils from your fingers don't ruin the paper. I had a pair of white gloves just like that for my first communion. My mother bought them at a department store downtown... Gimbel's, I think it was. Gimbel's had the most wonderful escalator, all shiny brass and wood. I used to ride it up and down while my mother looked at the perfume counters. Perfume gives me such a terrible headache now, especially those heavy floral ones. Roses are lovely in a garden, but bottled up, it's just too much. I tried planting a rose bush last spring, but the aphids got to it almost immediately. Little green bugs just covering the stems...\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you hear and the way your thoughts are connecting are related to your feelings of being radiant and furious, or are they separate things?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't think they're exactly the same thing. I mean, yes, lately I've felt so... so bright and electric, and the furious energy is there, but the voices don't just happen when I feel like that. Actually, there was a period just a little while ago, maybe for a few weeks, where I didn't feel energized or radiant at all. I was just perfectly normal, just quiet. But I still heard the women whispering in my apartment. And I knew, I absolutely knew, that the arts people were still monitoring my phone calls. \n\nPhones used to be so heavy, remember? The old rotary ones, the black ones. My parents had one in the hallway, and the cord was so long you could stretch it all the way into the kitchen if you wanted privacy. But it would get all tangled up like a corkscrew. Corkscrews are so tricky to use sometimes. I always break the cork when I try to open a bottle. My husband used to do it so easily, just a quick twist and pop. He had these wonderful, strong hands. He built a birdhouse once for the backyard, painted it bright red. The cardinals just loved it. Cardinals are such beautiful birds, the way that red pops against the snow in the winter. Snow is so peaceful when it first falls, before the plows come and turn it all gray and slushy...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected in a logical way when you're talking, or do they seem disjointed to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I think they make perfect sense to me, but... but I suppose other people don't always follow along. Like, my cousin will ask me a very simple question, just about a doctor's appointment or paying a utility bill, and I'll start trying to explain it. But then I have to explain the grant money for the archives, and that reminds me of the shipping routes they used to bring the photographs over, and then I'm telling her about my grandmother's altar because she had this beautiful old map on it. \n\nAltars are so fascinating, aren't they? The way people arrange things... candles and little statues. My grandmother had these tall, white taper candles that she'd light every Sunday. They smelled like beeswax. I love the smell of beeswax, it's so much nicer than those artificial vanilla candles you buy at the mall. The mall is always so crowded now, teenagers just loitering around the food court. I remember when the food court first opened, it was such a big deal. We went and got these huge slices of pizza on paper plates. Paper plates are so convenient, but they're terrible for the environment, all that trash piling up in landfills. Landfills just stretch on for miles, don't they? \n\nAnyway, by the time I stop talking to my cousin, she just looks at me and nobody seems to have any idea what I meant to say in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special or unique status that makes you more important or powerful than others in a way that's not based on your accomplishments or talents?\n\nPatient: Important? Oh, no, not at all. I don't have any special power or anything like that. I'm just a regular woman trying to do my archiving work. The archives are important, of course, because history is so fragile, but I'm just the one organizing it. It's just moments captured on these little pieces of paper. \n\nMy father used to take photographs with this heavy silver camera he bought after the war. It had those big flashbulbs that would pop and leave a purple spot in your vision for ten minutes afterwards. We used to go to the beach in New Jersey every summer, and he'd take pictures of us building sandcastles. The sand there was always so hot on your bare feet, you had to run all the way down to the water just to keep your toes from burning. The water was freezing, though, just shockingly cold. I used to find these tiny, perfect little white seashells in the tide pools and keep them in an old jelly jar on my bedroom windowsill. Jelly jars used to be so thick, the glass was practically unbreakable. You could drop them on the linoleum and they'd just bounce. Now everything comes in plastic. Plastic just feels so cheap, doesn't it? And it's everywhere now, just floating out there in the ocean...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected in a logical way when you're talking, or do they seem disjointed to you?\n\nPatient: I... didn't I just answer this? I feel like we just went over this a moment ago. \n\nLike I was saying, to me, everything connects perfectly fine. But I know it doesn't always sound that way to other people. I was just telling you about my cousin—if she asks me a simple question about paying a bill or an upcoming appointment, I'll start explaining the archive grant to her, and then that brings up the old shipping routes, and then I'm talking about my grandmother's altar. And by the time I stop talking, she just looks at me and nobody seems to know what I meant in the first place. \n\nBut those shipping routes really were quite dangerous back then, you know. The storms out on the Atlantic could just swallow a boat whole. I went on a boat once, a ferry across to Staten Island. The wind was so fierce it blew my scarf right off my neck and into the water. It was a beautiful silk scarf, hand-painted with little blue flowers. Silk is so delicate, you really have to hand-wash it in cold water in the sink. If you put it in the washing machine, it just ruins the fibers completely. Washing machines are so loud anyway, mine has this terrible spin cycle that shakes the whole kitchen floor. It makes the floorboards rattle so much I think the tea cups are going to fall right out of the cupboards. Cupboards in those old apartments were built so solid, though, real wood, not like the particle board they use today...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt extremely energetic or irritable for an extended period, like days or weeks, without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Well, energetic, yes. I get these days... sometimes it stretches on, and I just feel radiant. Like I'm chosen, you know? Like I'm glowing from the inside out and I have this incredible plan for the archives. I can work for hours and hours. But then, if someone questions my plan, or if my cousin tells me I need to slow down, I become absolutely furious. It's like they're trying to erase me. Erasing things is so dangerous, especially with historical documents. You use those little pink erasers, the ones that come on the ends of pencils, and they leave those terrible pink smudges all over the paper. Pencils are better than pens for archiving, though, because ink can bleed right through the paper. I bought this beautiful fountain pen once, it had a gold nib and real blue-black ink. The ink came in this little glass bottle with a cork stopper. Cork is fascinating, isn't it? It comes from the bark of a tree, they just peel it right off. I saw a documentary about it on PBS once. They have such good shows on PBS, the nature ones with the British narrators. Their voices are just so soothing, like a warm cup of tea on a rainy afternoon. Rain always makes my joints ache a little, though, right in the knees. It's the barometric pressure dropping...\n\nDoctor: Do you always have trouble keeping your thoughts on one subject, or are there times when your thoughts seem more organized and easier to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, it really depends. Sometimes I can sit down and just focus, but other times... it's like everything is pulling at me at once. If I try to read a letter for the archives, or even just sit and talk with my cousin, the slightest thing will pull me away. A siren going by outside, or the hum of the refrigerator kicking on, or even just a bright color on a brochure sitting on the table... I just lose the thread almost immediately. \n\nBrochures are always so glossy, aren't they? They use that heavy coated paper so the colors pop. I remember picking up a brochure for a botanical garden once, it had the most vibrant picture of a blue hydrangea on the front. Hydrangeas are fascinating because the color of the flowers changes depending on the acidity of the soil. If you put coffee grounds in the dirt, they turn blue, but if the soil is alkaline, they turn pink. My mother grew the most beautiful pink roses along the side of our house. They had thorns like little daggers, though. You had to wear these thick leather gloves just to prune them. Leather always smells so distinct, like an old library or a saddle shop. I bought a leather satchel at a flea market years ago to carry my notebooks, but the strap broke right in the middle of a crosswalk. The light changed and all the cars started honking, and I was just standing there trying to gather up all my loose papers before they blew away in the wind...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If I try to read a letter, or, you know, just sit down and talk with my cousin... she comes over on Tuesdays, usually... but the moment we start talking, a siren will go off outside, or I'll hear the refrigerator hum, or I'll see a bright color in a brochure on the table, and I just... I lose the thread almost immediately. It's like my mind just gets pulled right out the window. \n\nWindows are so drafty in my house. It's an older house, built in the twenties, I think. They used that beautiful wavy glass back then, the kind that distorts everything just a little bit when you look through it. I always loved looking out the window when it rained, watching the drops race each other down the glass. We had this terrible storm last spring that knocked a branch right through the living room window. Shattered everywhere. It took the repairman three weeks to come out and fix it, and he left muddy boot prints all over my good rug. It's a Persian rug, hand-woven. The colors are mostly deep blues and golds... gold is such a soft metal, isn't it? You can practically bend it with your bare hands if it's pure enough. My grandmother had a pure gold locket she wore every day of her life...\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a special or unique status that sets you apart from other people in a way that makes you more important or powerful than others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't feel like I'm more important or powerful than anyone else. I'm just me, you know? I don't have any grand powers. I can't even get the dandelions out of my front lawn. They just keep coming back, no matter how much I pull them. Dandelions are actually edible, you know. I read a book once about foraging, and they said you can make wine out of the flowers. Wine making seems like such an exact science, all those barrels and the fermenting process. We took a tour of a vineyard in Napa Valley for our anniversary one year. The smell of the grapes was so sweet, almost overpowering. They had these massive oak barrels stacked to the ceiling. Oak is such a sturdy wood, perfect for furniture. My dining room table is solid oak, weighs an absolute ton. We had to hire three men just to get it through the front door when we moved in. Moving is always such a nightmare, packing up your whole life into little cardboard boxes...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question whether the voices or instructions you receive might be a product of your own mind rather than an external reality?\n\nPatient: Well, no, how could they be my own mind? They're real women. I hear them so clearly in my apartment, and... and sometimes when I'm walking through empty museum rooms. They whisper from right behind the walls, telling me exactly which photographs belong in the archive. Archiving is such delicate work, you know. You have to wear those little white cotton gloves so the oils from your fingers don't ruin the paper. I had a pair of white gloves just like that for my first communion. My mother bought them at a department store downtown... Gimbel's, I think it was. Gimbel's had the most wonderful escalator, all shiny brass and wood. I used to ride it up and down while my mother looked at the perfume counters. Perfume gives me such a terrible headache now, especially those heavy floral ones. Roses are lovely in a garden, but bottled up, it's just too much. I tried planting a rose bush last spring, but the aphids got to it almost immediately. Little green bugs just covering the stems...\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you hear and the way your thoughts are connecting are related to your feelings of being radiant and furious, or are they separate things?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't think they're exactly the same thing. I mean, yes, lately I've felt so... so bright and electric, and the furious energy is there, but the voices don't just happen when I feel like that. Actually, there was a period just a little while ago, maybe for a few weeks, where I didn't feel energized or radiant at all. I was just perfectly normal, just quiet. But I still heard the women whispering in my apartment. And I knew, I absolutely knew, that the arts people were still monitoring my phone calls. \n\nPhones used to be so heavy, remember? The old rotary ones, the black ones. My parents had one in the hallway, and the cord was so long you could stretch it all the way into the kitchen if you wanted privacy. But it would get all tangled up like a corkscrew. Corkscrews are so tricky to use sometimes. I always break the cork when I try to open a bottle. My husband used to do it so easily, just a quick twist and pop. He had these wonderful, strong hands. He built a birdhouse once for the backyard, painted it bright red. The cardinals just loved it. Cardinals are such beautiful birds, the way that red pops against the snow in the winter. Snow is so peaceful when it first falls, before the plows come and turn it all gray and slushy...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected in a logical way when you're talking, or do they seem disjointed to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I think they make perfect sense to me, but... but I suppose other people don't always follow along. Like, my cousin will ask me a very simple question, just about a doctor's appointment or paying a utility bill, and I'll start trying to explain it. But then I have to explain the grant money for the archives, and that reminds me of the shipping routes they used to bring the photographs over, and then I'm telling her about my grandmother's altar because she had this beautiful old map on it. \n\nAltars are so fascinating, aren't they? The way people arrange things... candles and little statues. My grandmother had these tall, white taper candles that she'd light every Sunday. They smelled like beeswax. I love the smell of beeswax, it's so much nicer than those artificial vanilla candles you buy at the mall. The mall is always so crowded now, teenagers just loitering around the food court. I remember when the food court first opened, it was such a big deal. We went and got these huge slices of pizza on paper plates. Paper plates are so convenient, but they're terrible for the environment, all that trash piling up in landfills. Landfills just stretch on for miles, don't they? \n\nAnyway, by the time I stop talking to my cousin, she just looks at me and nobody seems to have any idea what I meant to say in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special or unique status that makes you more important or powerful than others in a way that's not based on your accomplishments or talents?\n\nPatient: Important? Oh, no, not at all. I don't have any special power or anything like that. I'm just a regular woman trying to do my archiving work. The archives are important, of course, because history is so fragile, but I'm just the one organizing it. It's just moments captured on these little pieces of paper. \n\nMy father used to take photographs with this heavy silver camera he bought after the war. It had those big flashbulbs that would pop and leave a purple spot in your vision for ten minutes afterwards. We used to go to the beach in New Jersey every summer, and he'd take pictures of us building sandcastles. The sand there was always so hot on your bare feet, you had to run all the way down to the water just to keep your toes from burning. The water was freezing, though, just shockingly cold. I used to find these tiny, perfect little white seashells in the tide pools and keep them in an old jelly jar on my bedroom windowsill. Jelly jars used to be so thick, the glass was practically unbreakable. You could drop them on the linoleum and they'd just bounce. Now everything comes in plastic. Plastic just feels so cheap, doesn't it? And it's everywhere now, just floating out there in the ocean...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected in a logical way when you're talking, or do they seem disjointed to you?\n\nPatient: I... didn't I just answer this? I feel like we just went over this a moment ago. \n\nLike I was saying, to me, everything connects perfectly fine. But I know it doesn't always sound that way to other people. I was just telling you about my cousin—if she asks me a simple question about paying a bill or an upcoming appointment, I'll start explaining the archive grant to her, and then that brings up the old shipping routes, and then I'm talking about my grandmother's altar. And by the time I stop talking, she just looks at me and nobody seems to know what I meant in the first place. \n\nBut those shipping routes really were quite dangerous back then, you know. The storms out on the Atlantic could just swallow a boat whole. I went on a boat once, a ferry across to Staten Island. The wind was so fierce it blew my scarf right off my neck and into the water. It was a beautiful silk scarf, hand-painted with little blue flowers. Silk is so delicate, you really have to hand-wash it in cold water in the sink. If you put it in the washing machine, it just ruins the fibers completely. Washing machines are so loud anyway, mine has this terrible spin cycle that shakes the whole kitchen floor. It makes the floorboards rattle so much I think the tea cups are going to fall right out of the cupboards. Cupboards in those old apartments were built so solid, though, real wood, not like the particle board they use today...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt extremely energetic or irritable for an extended period, like days or weeks, without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Well, energetic, yes. I get these days... sometimes it stretches on, and I just feel radiant. Like I'm chosen, you know? Like I'm glowing from the inside out and I have this incredible plan for the archives. I can work for hours and hours. But then, if someone questions my plan, or if my cousin tells me I need to slow down, I become absolutely furious. It's like they're trying to erase me. Erasing things is so dangerous, especially with historical documents. You use those little pink erasers, the ones that come on the ends of pencils, and they leave those terrible pink smudges all over the paper. Pencils are better than pens for archiving, though, because ink can bleed right through the paper. I bought this beautiful fountain pen once, it had a gold nib and real blue-black ink. The ink came in this little glass bottle with a cork stopper. Cork is fascinating, isn't it? It comes from the bark of a tree, they just peel it right off. I saw a documentary about it on PBS once. They have such good shows on PBS, the nature ones with the British narrators. Their voices are just so soothing, like a warm cup of tea on a rainy afternoon. Rain always makes my joints ache a little, though, right in the knees. It's the barometric pressure dropping...\n\nDoctor: Do you always have trouble keeping your thoughts on one subject, or are there times when your thoughts seem more organized and easier to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, it really depends. Sometimes I can sit down and just focus, but other times... it's like everything is pulling at me at once. If I try to read a letter for the archives, or even just sit and talk with my cousin, the slightest thing will pull me away. A siren going by outside, or the hum of the refrigerator kicking on, or even just a bright color on a brochure sitting on the table... I just lose the thread almost immediately. \n\nBrochures are always so glossy, aren't they? They use that heavy coated paper so the colors pop. I remember picking up a brochure for a botanical garden once, it had the most vibrant picture of a blue hydrangea on the front. Hydrangeas are fascinating because the color of the flowers changes depending on the acidity of the soil. If you put coffee grounds in the dirt, they turn blue, but if the soil is alkaline, they turn pink. My mother grew the most beautiful pink roses along the side of our house. They had thorns like little daggers, though. You had to wear these thick leather gloves just to prune them. Leather always smells so distinct, like an old library or a saddle shop. I bought a leather satchel at a flea market years ago to carry my notebooks, but the strap broke right in the middle of a crosswalk. The light changed and all the cars started honking, and I was just standing there trying to gather up all my loose papers before they blew away in the wind..."
